### Question 71: [Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism]
- **Difficulty**: [Medium]
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: [The question tests the understanding of the physiological effects of catecholamines, specifically epinephrine, on cardiac function, mediated by beta-1 adrenergic receptor activation, including positive chronotropy (increased heart rate), positive dromotropy (increased conduction velocity), and positive inotropy (increased contractility) via PKA-mediated phosphorylation of specific ion channels and proteins in cardiac myocytes and nodal cells. It also assesses the ability to differentiate the effects of beta-1 stimulation from other adrenergic receptor subtypes or catecholamines.]

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 23-year-old male undergoes an electrophysiology study to evaluate supraventricular tachycardia. Baseline ECG shows a normal sinus rhythm with a heart rate of 65 bpm and a PR interval of 160 ms. During the study, an intravenous infusion of isoproterenol is initiated. Within minutes, the heart rate increases to 130 bpm, the PR interval shortens to 110 ms, and hemodynamic monitoring reveals an 80% increase in the maximum rate of left ventricular pressure rise (dP/dt max). Arterial blood pressure remains relatively stable. Which of the following intracellular signaling cascades is MOST directly responsible for the observed increase in cardiac contractility (dP/dt max)?

(A) Activation of phospholipase C (PLC) leading to increased intracellular calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum.
(B) Inhibition of adenylyl cyclase (AC) leading to decreased cyclic AMP (cAMP) levels and reduced phosphorylation of L-type calcium channels.
(C) Activation of Gs protein leading to increased cAMP levels and subsequent activation of protein kinase A (PKA).
(D) Activation of Gq protein leading to increased phosphatidylinositol 4,5-bisphosphate (PIP2) hydrolysis and diacylglycerol (DAG) production.
(E) Activation of Gβγ subunits leading to direct opening of potassium channels and hyperpolarization of cardiac myocytes.

#### Correct Answer: [C]

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 23-year-old male undergoing electrophysiology study. This context suggests cardiac function assessment.
- **Baseline:** Normal sinus rhythm, HR 65 bpm, PR 160 ms. Provides a baseline for comparison.
- **Intervention:** Intravenous infusion of isoproterenol. Isoproterenol is a non-selective beta-adrenergic agonist (β1, β2, β3).
- **Effects:**
    - HR increases from 65 to 130 bpm (positive chronotropy).
    - PR interval shortens from 160 to 110 ms (positive dromotropy).
    - dP/dt max increases by 80% (positive inotropy).
    - BP remains stable (suggests balanced vasodilation and increased cardiac output, consistent with beta-2 effects counteracting alpha-1 effects or beta-1 effects on renin release).
- **Question:** Mechanism responsible for increased contractility (dP/dt max). This focuses specifically on the inotropic effect.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Isoproterenol Action:** Isoproterenol binds to beta-adrenergic receptors (β1, β2, β3). In the heart, β1 receptors are predominant on sinoatrial (SA) nodal cells, atrioventricular (AV) nodal cells, and ventricular myocytes.
- **Receptor Coupling:** Beta-adrenergic receptors are Gs protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs).
- **Gs Protein Activation:** Binding of isoproterenol activates the Gs protein, causing it to dissociate into Gαs and Gβγ subunits.
- **Adenylyl Cyclase (AC) Activation:** The Gαs subunit activates adenylyl cyclase (AC).
- **cAMP Production:** Activated AC catalyzes the conversion of ATP to cyclic AMP (cAMP). This leads to a significant increase in intracellular cAMP levels.
- **Protein Kinase A (PKA) Activation:** Increased cAMP binds to and activates protein kinase A (PKA). PKA is a serine/threonine kinase.
- **Cardiac Contractility (Inotropy):** PKA phosphorylates several key proteins involved in cardiac muscle contraction:
    - **L-type Calcium Channels (Cav1.2):** Phosphorylation increases the influx of Ca2+ during the plateau phase of the action potential, enhancing intracellular Ca2+ concentration.
    - **Ryanodine Receptors (RyR2) on the Sarcoplasmic Reticulum (SR):** Phosphorylation increases the sensitivity of RyR2 channels to Ca2+, leading to greater Ca2+ release from the SR during the action potential.
    - **Phospholamban:** Phosphorylation inactivates phospholamban, which normally inhibits the SERCA2a pump (sarco/endoplasmic reticulum Ca2+-ATPase). Inactivation of phospholamban increases the activity of SERCA2a, leading to faster reuptake of Ca2+ into the SR, which enhances the speed and magnitude of subsequent Ca2+ release.
    - **Troponin I:** Phosphorylation increases the sensitivity of troponin C to Ca2+, enhancing the interaction between actin and myosin filaments.
- **Net Effect on Contractility:** The combined effect of increased Ca2+ influx, increased Ca2+ release from the SR, faster Ca2+ reuptake, and increased troponin sensitivity is a significant increase in the force of myocardial contraction, reflected by the increased dP/dt max.
- **Chronotropy:** PKA phosphorylates HCN channels (If) in SA nodal cells, increasing the rate of diastolic depolarization and thus increasing heart rate. It also phosphorylates T-type Ca2+ channels, contributing to the pacemaker potential.
- **Dromotropy:** PKA phosphorylates L-type Ca2+ channels in AV nodal cells, increasing Ca2+ influx and shortening the action potential duration, which speeds up conduction velocity through the AV node (shortening the PR interval).

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The patient receives isoproterenol, a beta-adrenergic agonist.
2.  Isoproterenol acts on beta-1 receptors in the heart.
3.  Beta-1 receptors are coupled to Gs proteins.
4.  Gs protein activation leads to activation of adenylyl cyclase (AC).
5.  AC activation increases intracellular cAMP levels.
6.  Increased cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA).
7.  PKA phosphorylates key proteins involved in cardiac contraction (L-type Ca2+ channels, RyR2, phospholamban, troponin I).
8.  Phosphorylation of these proteins increases intracellular Ca2+ availability and enhances the sensitivity of the contractile apparatus to Ca2+.
9.  This results in increased myocardial contractility (positive inotropy), observed as an increase in dP/dt max.
10. The question asks for the intracellular signaling cascade MOST directly responsible for the increased contractility. The cascade involving Gs activation, cAMP increase, and PKA activation is the primary pathway mediating beta-1 receptor effects on contractility.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Activation of phospholipase C (PLC) leading to increased intracellular calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum.** PLC activation is associated with Gq protein-coupled receptors (e.g., alpha-1 adrenergic receptors, M1 muscarinic receptors). This pathway increases IP3 and DAG, leading to Ca2+ release from the SR via IP3 receptors. While this increases intracellular Ca2+, it is not the primary mechanism for beta-1 adrenergic stimulation. Beta-1 receptors are coupled to Gs, not Gq.
- **(B) Inhibition of adenylyl cyclase (AC) leading to decreased cyclic AMP (cAMP) levels and reduced phosphorylation of L-type calcium channels.** Inhibition of AC and decreased cAMP levels are characteristic of Gi protein-coupled receptors (e.g., alpha-2 adrenergic receptors, M2 muscarinic receptors). This would decrease PKA activity and reduce contractility, opposite to the observed effect of isoproterenol.
- **(C) Activation of Gs protein leading to increased cAMP levels and subsequent activation of protein kinase A (PKA).** This accurately describes the signaling pathway initiated by beta-1 adrenergic receptor activation. The subsequent PKA-mediated phosphorylation of various proteins directly leads to increased cardiac contractility (inotropy), heart rate (chronotropy), and conduction velocity (dromotropy), all observed in the vignette. This is the correct mechanism.
- **(D) Activation of Gq protein leading to increased phosphatidylinositol 4,5-bisphosphate (PIP2) hydrolysis and diacylglycerol (DAG) production.** Gq protein activation is associated with receptors like alpha-1 adrenergic receptors. This pathway leads to increased intracellular Ca2+ via IP3 receptors but is not the mechanism for beta-1 adrenergic stimulation.
- **(E) Activation of Gβγ subunits leading to direct opening of potassium channels and hyperpolarization of cardiac myocytes.** While Gβγ subunits can have various effects, including modulating ion channels, the primary signaling pathway for beta-1 receptor-mediated increases in contractility, heart rate, and conduction involves Gαs activation of AC and subsequent cAMP/PKA signaling. Direct Gβγ-mediated K+ channel opening would lead to hyperpolarization and decreased excitability, not increased contractility.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Receptor Type | G Protein | Effector Enzyme | Second Messenger | Primary Cardiac Effects (Beta-1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Beta-1 (β1) | Gs | Adenylyl Cyclase (AC) | cAMP | ↑ Chronotropy, ↑ Dromotropy, ↑ Inotropy |
| Beta-2 (β2) | Gs | Adenylyl Cyclase (AC) | cAMP | Vasodilation (skeletal muscle), Bronchodilation, ↑ Glycogenolysis |
| Alpha-1 (α1) | Gq | Phospholipase C (PLC) | IP3, DAG | Vasoconstriction, ↑ SVR, ↑ BP |
| Alpha-2 (α2) | Gi | Inhibition of AC | ↓ cAMP | Presynaptic inhibition, ↓ NE release, ↓ Insulin release |
| Muscarinic M2 | Gi | Inhibition of AC | ↓ cAMP | ↓ Chronotropy, ↓ Dromotropy, ↓ Inotropy |
| Muscarinic M1 | Gq | Phospholipase C (PLC) | IP3, DAG | ↑ AV node Ca2+ current, ↑ Dromotropy (at high doses) |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Beta-1 adrenergic receptor activation by catecholamines like epinephrine or isoproterenol initiates a Gs protein-dependent signaling cascade, leading to increased cAMP production and subsequent PKA activation. PKA phosphorylates key proteins in cardiac myocytes, enhancing calcium handling and increasing the force of contraction (positive inotropy), heart rate (positive chronotropy), and conduction velocity (positive dromotropy). Understanding this Gs-cAMP-PKA pathway is crucial for comprehending the effects of sympathetic stimulation on the heart.

---

### Question 72: [Adrenergic Pharmacology / Cardiac Physiology / Positive Lusitropy Mechanism]
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The mechanism by which beta-1 adrenergic receptor stimulation enhances myocardial relaxation (positive lusitropy) via PKA-mediated phosphorylation of phospholamban, leading to increased SERCA2a activity and accelerated calcium reuptake into the sarcoplasmic reticulum.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of hypertension and atrial fibrillation presents to the emergency department complaining of palpitations and shortness of breath. His heart rate is 160 bpm, blood pressure is 140/90 mmHg, and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. An ECG shows rapid atrial fibrillation with a ventricular response rate of 160 bpm. A cardiology fellow performs high-frequency pressure-volume (PV) loop analysis during this rapid heart rate. The PV loops reveal a significantly shortened cardiac cycle duration, consistent with the high heart rate. However, despite the rapid cycle, the end-diastolic volume (EDV) and end-diastolic pressure (EDP) are relatively preserved, indicating adequate ventricular filling. The rate of ventricular pressure decline (dP/dt min) during isovolumetric relaxation is markedly increased compared to baseline PV loops obtained at a slower heart rate. The fellow notes that this enhanced relaxation, allowing for preserved filling despite the shortened cycle, is characteristic of positive lusitropy. Laboratory tests show elevated plasma norepinephrine levels (1200 pg/mL; normal < 80 pg/mL). The patient is subsequently treated with intravenous metoprolol, a beta-1 selective antagonist. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed positive lusitropy during high adrenergic stimulation?

(A) Increased L-type calcium channel (Cav1.2) conductance, leading to greater calcium influx during systole and enhanced calcium-induced calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR).
(B) Phosphorylation of troponin I by PKA, increasing the sensitivity of the contractile apparatus to intracellular calcium.
(C) Phosphorylation of phospholamban by PKA, relieving its inhibition of SERCA2a and accelerating calcium reuptake into the SR.
(D) Activation of phosphodiesterase 3 (PDE3) by PKA, leading to decreased cAMP levels and reduced calcium sensitivity of the contractile proteins.
(E) Increased activity of the Na+/Ca2+ exchanger (NCX) due to elevated intracellular sodium levels, facilitating calcium extrusion from the cell.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old male, hypertension, atrial fibrillation, palpitations, shortness of breath, tachycardia (160 bpm). Suggests a state of high sympathetic tone or adrenergic excess.
- **High Norepinephrine Levels:** Confirms significant adrenergic stimulation.
- **PV Loop Analysis:** Shortened cardiac cycle (expected with tachycardia). Preserved EDV/EDP despite rapid cycle. Markedly increased rate of ventricular pressure decline (dP/dt min during relaxation). These findings point to enhanced myocardial relaxation (positive lusitropy).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism underlying the observed positive lusitropy during high adrenergic stimulation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Adrenergic Stimulation & Cardiac Effects:** Beta-1 adrenergic receptors are predominantly found in the heart. Activation by catecholamines (like norepinephrine and epinephrine) leads to Gs protein activation, increased adenylyl cyclase activity, elevated intracellular cAMP, and activation of Protein Kinase A (PKA).
- **PKA Targets in Cardiomyocytes:** PKA phosphorylates multiple target proteins involved in cardiac contractility and relaxation. Key targets include:
    - **L-type Calcium Channels (Cav1.2):** Phosphorylation increases calcium influx during the action potential plateau, enhancing contractility (positive inotropy).
    - **Ryanodine Receptors (RyR2):** Phosphorylation increases the sensitivity of the SR to calcium release, enhancing calcium-induced calcium release (CICR) and contractility.
    - **Troponin I:** Phosphorylation increases the sensitivity of the contractile apparatus (actin-myosin interaction) to calcium, enhancing contractility.
    - **Phospholamban (PLN):** This is the key target for lusitropy. Phospholamban is a protein that inhibits the activity of the Sarcoplasmic/Endoplasmic Reticulum Calcium ATPase (SERCA2a). SERCA2a is the primary pump responsible for sequestering calcium from the cytosol back into the SR during diastole.
    - **Myofilament Proteins (e.g., MLC2V, Tropomyosin):** Phosphorylation can modulate calcium sensitivity, but the primary effect on relaxation is via SERCA2a regulation.
- **Phospholamban & SERCA2a:** Unphosphorylated phospholamban binds to and inhibits SERCA2a, slowing down the rate of calcium reuptake into the SR. When PKA phosphorylates phospholamban (specifically at Serine-16), it dissociates from SERCA2a, relieving the inhibition. This allows SERCA2a to pump calcium out of the cytosol and back into the SR much more rapidly.
- **Positive Lusitropy:** The accelerated calcium removal from the cytosol due to enhanced SERCA2a activity leads to a faster decline in intracellular calcium concentration during diastole. This results in faster relaxation of the myofibrils, a steeper decline in ventricular pressure (increased dP/dt min during relaxation), and improved diastolic filling. This phenomenon is termed positive lusitropy.
- **Other Options Analysis:**
    - (A) Increased Cav1.2 conductance contributes to positive inotropy, not primarily lusitropy. While calcium handling is involved, the *rate* of relaxation is the key feature here, driven by calcium *removal*.
    - (B) Troponin I phosphorylation enhances contractility (positive inotropy) by increasing calcium sensitivity, not relaxation.
    - (D) PDE3 activation leads to cAMP breakdown, which would *decrease* PKA activity and *reduce* both contractility and lusitropy.
    - (E) NCX activity is important for calcium homeostasis, but its primary role is calcium extrusion from the cell in exchange for sodium influx. While it contributes to relaxation, the dominant mechanism for rapid calcium removal *into the SR* during diastole, which is enhanced by beta-adrenergic stimulation, is SERCA2a activity regulated by phospholamban phosphorylation.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The patient presents with signs of adrenergic excess (tachycardia, palpitations, elevated norepinephrine).
2.  PV loop analysis shows enhanced myocardial relaxation (positive lusitropy) despite a rapid heart rate, indicated by preserved filling (EDV/EDP) and increased rate of pressure decline (dP/dt min).
3.  The question asks for the molecular mechanism of this positive lusitropy.
4.  Beta-1 adrenergic stimulation activates PKA.
5.  PKA phosphorylates several cardiac proteins.
6.  Phosphorylation of phospholamban by PKA relieves its inhibition of SERCA2a.
7.  Increased SERCA2a activity accelerates calcium reuptake into the SR.
8.  Faster calcium removal leads to faster relaxation (positive lusitropy).
9.  Therefore, the phosphorylation of phospholamban by PKA is the correct mechanism.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Increased L-type calcium channel (Cav1.2) conductance:** This is a known effect of beta-1 stimulation via PKA phosphorylation, but it primarily contributes to increased calcium influx during systole, leading to *positive inotropy* (increased contractility), not the enhanced *relaxation* (positive lusitropy) observed in the PV loops. The key finding is the rapid *pressure decline* during diastole, reflecting faster relaxation.
- **(B) Phosphorylation of troponin I by PKA:** Similar to option A, this enhances the sensitivity of the contractile proteins to calcium, leading to *positive inotropy*. It does not directly explain the accelerated relaxation phase.
- **(C) Phosphorylation of phospholamban by PKA, relieving its inhibition of SERCA2a and accelerating calcium reuptake into the SR:** This is the correct mechanism. PKA phosphorylates phospholamban, which then detaches from SERCA2a. This allows SERCA2a to pump calcium back into the SR more efficiently, leading to a faster decrease in cytosolic calcium concentration and thus faster myocardial relaxation (positive lusitropy). This directly explains the observed PV loop findings.
- **(D) Activation of phosphodiesterase 3 (PDE3) by PKA:** PKA *inhibits* PDE3, not activates it. Furthermore, PDE3 breaks down cAMP. Increased PDE3 activity would *decrease* cAMP levels, leading to *decreased* PKA activity and *reduced* contractility and lusitropy. This option is physiologically incorrect and describes the opposite effect.
- **(E) Increased activity of the Na+/Ca2+ exchanger (NCX):** While NCX contributes to calcium removal from the cell, its primary role is exchanging intracellular calcium for extracellular sodium. Its contribution to the rapid relaxation phase, especially the enhanced relaxation seen with beta-stimulation, is less significant than the SERCA2a-mediated calcium reuptake into the SR, which is directly regulated by phospholamban phosphorylation. The dominant mechanism for positive lusitropy in this context is SERCA2a enhancement.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Mechanism                                  | Target Protein | Effect on Relaxation (Lusitropy) | Effect on Contraction (Inotropy) | Mediated by |
| :----------------------------------------- | :------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------- | :---------- |
| Phospholamban Phosphorylation              | Phospholamban  | Increased (Positive)            | No direct effect                 | PKA         |
| SERCA2a Activity (indirectly via PLN)      | SERCA2a        | Increased (Positive)            | No direct effect                 | PKA         |
| L-type Ca Channel Phosphorylation         | Cav1.2         | No direct effect                 | Increased (Positive)             | PKA         |
| Troponin I Phosphorylation                 | Troponin I     | No direct effect                 | Increased (Positive)             | PKA         |
| RyR2 Phosphorylation                       | RyR2           | No direct effect                 | Increased (Positive)             | PKA         |
| PDE3 Activation (Incorrect)                | N/A            | Decreased                       | Decreased                        | PKA         |
| NCX Activity                               | NCX            | Minor contribution              | Minor contribution              | N/A         |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Beta-1 adrenergic stimulation enhances both cardiac contractility (positive inotropy) and relaxation (positive lusitropy). The positive inotropic effects are primarily mediated by increased calcium influx via L-type channels and increased calcium release from the SR via RyR2, along with increased calcium sensitivity of the contractile apparatus via troponin I phosphorylation. The positive lusitropic effect is primarily mediated by PKA-dependent phosphorylation of phospholamban, which relieves its inhibition of SERCA2a, leading to faster calcium reuptake into the SR and accelerated myocardial relaxation.

### Question 73: [Renal Physiology / Autonomic Nervous System / Adrenergic Pharmacology / Renin-Angiotensin-Aldosterone System (RAAS)]
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Beta-1 adrenergic receptor stimulation on juxtaglomerular (JG) cells increases renin release via Gs protein activation, leading to increased intracellular cAMP and exocytosis of renin granules. Beta-1 blockade suppresses renin release. (β1肾上腺素能受体刺激肾小球旁细胞，通过Gs蛋白激活，增加胞内cAMP，促进肾素颗粒胞吐，β1阻滞剂抑制肾素释放。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old male with a 10-year history of essential hypertension is initiated on metoprolol tartrate 50 mg twice daily. Metoprolol is a selective beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonist. Two weeks after starting metoprolol, his blood pressure is well-controlled (125/78 mmHg). Routine laboratory testing reveals a significant decrease in his plasma renin activity (PRA) by 65% compared to baseline measurements taken before starting metoprolol. Concurrently, his plasma angiotensin II levels have decreased by 55%, and his plasma aldosterone levels have decreased by 45%. His serum potassium is within the normal range (4.2 mEq/L). Which of the following physiological mechanisms best explains the observed decrease in PRA following metoprolol administration?

(A) Metoprolol inhibits the conversion of angiotensin I to angiotensin II by blocking beta-1 receptors on angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE).
(B) Metoprolol stimulates the release of atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP), which directly inhibits renin secretion from JG cells.
(C) Metoprolol blocks beta-1 adrenergic receptors on the JG cells of the afferent arteriole, thereby decreasing renin synthesis and release.
(D) Metoprolol increases renal blood flow to the afferent arteriole, leading to decreased baroreceptor stimulation and subsequent renin release inhibition.
(E) Metoprolol enhances the activity of the sympathetic nervous system, leading to increased norepinephrine release, which stimulates renin release via beta-1 receptors.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 48-year-old male with essential hypertension.
- **Treatment:** Metoprolol tartrate (selective beta-1 antagonist).
- **Outcome:** Blood pressure controlled.
- **Lab Findings:** Significant decrease in PRA (65%), plasma angiotensin II (55%), and plasma aldosterone (45%) after 2 weeks of metoprolol.
- **Question:** Mechanism explaining the decrease in PRA.
- **Key Clue:** The patient is on a beta-1 blocker, and PRA, angiotensin II, and aldosterone (all downstream products of renin) are decreased. This points directly to an effect of beta-1 blockade on renin release.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Renin Source:** Renin is primarily synthesized and secreted by the juxtaglomerular (JG) granular cells located in the wall of the afferent arteriole of the kidney.
- **JG Cell Regulation:** Renin release from JG cells is regulated by three main stimuli:
    1.  **Decreased Renal Perfusion Pressure:** Detected by baroreceptors in the afferent arteriole.
    2.  **Decreased NaCl Delivery to the Macula Densa:** Detected by macula densa cells in the distal convoluted tubule.
    3.  **Sympathetic Nervous System Activation:** Beta-1 adrenergic receptors are present on JG cells. Stimulation by norepinephrine (NE) or epinephrine (E) increases intracellular cyclic AMP (cAMP) via Gs protein activation, leading to exocytosis of renin-containing granules.
- **Metoprolol Mechanism:** Metoprolol is a selective beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonist. It competitively binds to beta-1 receptors, preventing the binding of endogenous catecholamines (NE, E).
- **Effect on Renin:** By blocking beta-1 receptors on JG cells, metoprolol inhibits the sympathetic stimulation of renin release. This leads to a decrease in renin secretion into the circulation, resulting in lower PRA. The subsequent decrease in angiotensin II (formed from angiotensinogen by renin) and aldosterone (stimulated by angiotensin II) are secondary consequences of reduced renin activity.
- **Other Options Analysis:**
    - (A) ACE is not directly regulated by beta-1 receptors. ACE converts Angiotensin I to Angiotensin II.
    - (B) ANP inhibits renin release, but metoprolol's primary mechanism is beta-1 blockade, not ANP stimulation.
    - (D) Metoprolol decreases heart rate and contractility, potentially reducing overall sympathetic tone, but its direct effect on JG cells via beta-1 blockade is the primary mechanism for decreased renin. Furthermore, decreased renal blood flow would *increase* renin release, not decrease it.
    - (E) Metoprolol *blocks* beta-1 receptors, thus *inhibiting* the stimulatory effect of catecholamines on renin release, not enhancing it.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the drug: Metoprolol is a selective beta-1 blocker.
2.  Identify the effect: PRA, angiotensin II, and aldosterone are decreased.
3.  Connect the drug to the effect: Beta-1 receptors are known to stimulate renin release from JG cells.
4.  Deduce the mechanism: Blocking beta-1 receptors on JG cells will inhibit renin release.
5.  Evaluate the options: Option (C) directly describes this mechanism. Options (A), (B), (D), and (E) describe incorrect or secondary mechanisms.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Metoprolol inhibits the conversion of angiotensin I to angiotensin II by blocking beta-1 receptors on angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE).** Incorrect. ACE is not directly regulated by adrenergic receptors. ACE inhibitors (like lisinopril) block ACE, but metoprolol is a beta-blocker.
- **(B) Metoprolol stimulates the release of atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP), which directly inhibits renin secretion from JG cells.** Incorrect. Metoprolol does not stimulate ANP release. ANP inhibits renin, but this is not the mechanism of metoprolol's effect.
- **(C) Metoprolol blocks beta-1 adrenergic receptors on the JG cells of the afferent arteriole, thereby decreasing renin synthesis and release.** Correct. This accurately describes the direct effect of a beta-1 blocker on renin secretion. Beta-1 stimulation increases renin release; blocking it decreases release.
- **(D) Metoprolol increases renal blood flow to the afferent arteriole, leading to decreased baroreceptor stimulation and subsequent renin release inhibition.** Incorrect. Metoprolol generally decreases cardiac output and systemic blood pressure, which might slightly decrease renal perfusion pressure, potentially *increasing* renin release via the baroreceptor mechanism, although the direct beta-1 blockade effect is dominant. Furthermore, increased renal blood flow would decrease renin release, but metoprolol does not typically increase renal blood flow significantly in this context.
- **(E) Metoprolol enhances the activity of the sympathetic nervous system, leading to increased norepinephrine release, which stimulates renin release via beta-1 receptors.** Incorrect. Metoprolol *blocks* beta-1 receptors, inhibiting the effects of norepinephrine. It does not enhance sympathetic activity or increase renin release.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Beta-1 Receptors on JG Cells | Metoprolol Effect                                  | Consequence                                  |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- |
| **Stimulus**             | Catecholamines (NE, E)       | Blockade                                          | Inhibition of renin release                  |
| **Signal Transduction** | Gs -> cAMP increase          | Prevents Gs activation by catecholamines          | Decreased cAMP, reduced exocytosis           |
| **Effect on Renin**     | Increases release            | Decreases release                                 | ↓ PRA, ↓ Angiotensin II, ↓ Aldosterone       |
| **Clinical Relevance**   | Target for beta-blockers     | Explains ↓ RAAS activity with beta-1 blockade     | Important side effect in hypertension tx     |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Selective beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonists like metoprolol block the stimulatory effect of catecholamines on renin release from juxtaglomerular cells, leading to decreased plasma renin activity and subsequent reduction in angiotensin II and aldosterone levels. This is a key mechanism underlying the antihypertensive effect and a common side effect of beta-blockers.

### Question 74: Beta-2 Adrenergic Receptor Signaling: Smooth Muscle Relaxation via MLCK Inactivation
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The mechanism of smooth muscle relaxation mediated by beta-2 adrenergic receptor activation, specifically the downstream signaling cascade involving Gs protein, adenylyl cyclase, cAMP, protein kinase A (PKA), and the inactivation of myosin light chain kinase (MLCK).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male with a history of severe, poorly controlled asthma presents to the emergency department with acute respiratory distress. His symptoms include wheezing, shortness of breath, and chest tightness. Vital signs show a respiratory rate of 30 breaths/min, heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, and SpO2 of 88% on room air. Arterial blood gas analysis reveals respiratory acidosis (pH 7.30, PaCO2 55 mmHg, PaO2 60 mmHg). The patient is immediately treated with inhaled albuterol, a short-acting beta-2 adrenergic agonist. Within minutes of administration, his wheezing decreases significantly, and his respiratory rate and SpO2 improve. An experimental pharmacologist is studying the mechanism of albuterol-induced bronchodilation using isolated human tracheal smooth muscle strips. The strips are pre-contracted with methacholine (a muscarinic agonist). The pharmacologist measures the tension generated by the smooth muscle. Addition of albuterol leads to a rapid and near-complete decrease in tension. Which of the following molecular events is the primary mechanism by which albuterol causes relaxation of the tracheal smooth muscle in this experimental setup?

(A) Activation of phospholipase C (PLC) leading to increased intracellular calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum.
(B) Activation of adenylyl cyclase via Gq protein, resulting in decreased cAMP levels and inhibition of protein kinase A (PKA).
(C) Activation of adenylyl cyclase via Gs protein, leading to increased cAMP levels and subsequent phosphorylation and inactivation of myosin light chain kinase (MLCK).
(D) Direct binding of albuterol to voltage-gated calcium channels, causing their closure and reducing calcium influx.
(E) Activation of Rho-associated protein kinase (ROCK), leading to increased phosphorylation of myosin light chains and sustained contraction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Asthma exacerbation with bronchoconstriction (wheezing, shortness of breath).
- **Treatment:** Albuterol (beta-2 agonist) administration leads to rapid improvement (bronchodilation).
- **Experimental Setup:** Isolated tracheal smooth muscle strips pre-contracted with methacholine (mimicking cholinergic bronchoconstriction).
- **Observation:** Albuterol causes rapid and near-complete relaxation (decreased tension).
- **Question:** Asks for the *primary molecular mechanism* of albuterol-induced relaxation in this context.
- **Key Clue:** Albuterol is a beta-2 agonist, and the effect is smooth muscle relaxation (bronchodilation). This points towards the signaling pathway activated by beta-2 receptors that leads to smooth muscle relaxation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Beta-2 Adrenergic Receptors:** These are G protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs) primarily coupled to the stimulatory G protein (Gs).
- **Gs Protein Activation:** When albuterol binds to the beta-2 receptor, the receptor undergoes a conformational change, activating the associated Gs protein.
- **Adenylyl Cyclase Activation:** The activated alpha subunit (Gsα) of the Gs protein stimulates the enzyme adenylyl cyclase.
- **cAMP Production:** Adenylyl cyclase catalyzes the conversion of ATP to cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP). This leads to a significant increase in intracellular cAMP levels.
- **Protein Kinase A (PKA) Activation:** Increased cAMP levels activate protein kinase A (PKA). PKA is a serine/threonine kinase that phosphorylates various target proteins.
- **MLCK Phosphorylation and Inactivation:** In smooth muscle cells, PKA phosphorylates myosin light chain kinase (MLCK). Phosphorylation of MLCK by PKA significantly reduces its affinity for the calcium-calmodulin complex.
- **Myosin Light Chain Phosphorylation:** MLCK is responsible for phosphorylating the regulatory light chain of myosin. Phosphorylation of myosin light chains is required for myosin to interact with actin and generate force (contraction).
- **Smooth Muscle Relaxation:** By inactivating MLCK, PKA prevents or reduces the phosphorylation of myosin light chains, even in the presence of calcium. This leads to decreased myosin-actin interaction and smooth muscle relaxation.
- **Other PKA Effects:** PKA also phosphorylates and activates other ion channels, such as calcium-activated potassium channels (BK_Ca) and Na+/Ca2+ exchangers, which further contribute to smooth muscle relaxation by promoting potassium efflux and calcium extrusion, respectively. However, the primary mechanism for *reducing* contractile force is the inactivation of MLCK.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has asthma and responds to albuterol, a beta-2 agonist.
2. The experimental setup confirms albuterol causes tracheal smooth muscle relaxation.
3. The question asks for the molecular mechanism of this relaxation.
4. Beta-2 receptors are coupled to Gs proteins.
5. Gs activation leads to increased cAMP via adenylyl cyclase.
6. Increased cAMP activates PKA.
7. PKA phosphorylates and inactivates MLCK.
8. Inactivation of MLCK reduces myosin light chain phosphorylation.
9. Reduced myosin light chain phosphorylation leads to smooth muscle relaxation.
10. Therefore, the primary mechanism is the Gs -> cAMP -> PKA -> MLCK inactivation pathway.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Activation of phospholipase C (PLC) leading to increased intracellular calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum.** PLC activation is characteristic of Gq-coupled receptors (e.g., alpha-1 adrenergic, muscarinic M1/M3). This pathway *increases* intracellular calcium, leading to contraction, not relaxation. Beta-2 receptors are coupled to Gs, not Gq. Incorrect.
- **(B) Activation of adenylyl cyclase via Gq protein, resulting in decreased cAMP levels and inhibition of protein kinase A (PKA).** Beta-2 receptors are coupled to Gs, not Gq. Gq activation leads to PLC activation, not adenylyl cyclase activation. Furthermore, Gs activation *increases* cAMP and activates PKA, the opposite of what is stated. Incorrect.
- **(C) Activation of adenylyl cyclase via Gs protein, leading to increased cAMP levels and subsequent phosphorylation and inactivation of myosin light chain kinase (MLCK).** This accurately describes the signaling cascade initiated by beta-2 receptor activation. Gs activates adenylyl cyclase, increasing cAMP, which activates PKA. PKA phosphorylates and inactivates MLCK, leading to smooth muscle relaxation. Correct.
- **(D) Direct binding of albuterol to voltage-gated calcium channels, causing their closure and reducing calcium influx.** Albuterol acts via beta-2 adrenergic receptors (GPCRs), not directly on voltage-gated calcium channels. While reducing calcium influx contributes to relaxation, it is not the primary mechanism of beta-2 agonist action. Incorrect.
- **(E) Activation of Rho-associated protein kinase (ROCK), leading to increased phosphorylation of myosin light chains and sustained contraction.** ROCK activation leads to smooth muscle contraction, often via phosphorylation of MLCK or myosin light chains themselves, increasing tension. This is the opposite effect of albuterol. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Receptor/Enzyme | G Protein Coupling | Second Messenger | Effect on Smooth Muscle | Key Mechanism |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Alpha-1 | Gq | IP3, DAG | Contraction | Increased intracellular Ca2+ via IP3; PKC activation |
| Alpha-2 | Gi | Decreased cAMP | Contraction (usually) / Relaxation (some) | Inhibition of AC; K+ channel opening |
| Beta-1 | Gs | cAMP | Contraction (some) / Relaxation (some) | PKA activation; MLCK phosphorylation (complex) |
| Beta-2 | Gs | cAMP | Relaxation | PKA activation; MLCK phosphorylation/inactivation; K+ channel opening; Na+/Ca2+ exchanger activation |
| Beta-3 | Gs | cAMP | Relaxation | PKA activation; Lipolysis |
| Adenylyl Cyclase | Gs | cAMP | Relaxation | PKA activation |
| Phospholipase C | Gq | IP3, DAG | Contraction | Increased intracellular Ca2+ |
| MLCK | - | - | Contraction | Phosphorylates myosin light chains |
| PKA | cAMP | - | Relaxation | Phosphorylates MLCK (inactivation); phosphorylates K+ channels, Na+/Ca2+ exchanger |
| ROCK | - | - | Contraction | Phosphorylates MLCK, myosin light chains |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Beta-2 adrenergic receptor activation initiates a signaling cascade involving Gs protein, adenylyl cyclase, cAMP, and PKA. The primary mechanism of smooth muscle relaxation mediated by this pathway is the PKA-mediated phosphorylation and inactivation of myosin light chain kinase (MLCK), which prevents myosin light chain phosphorylation and subsequent cross-bridge cycling, leading to decreased tension. Understanding this specific pathway is crucial for pharmacology and physiology related to bronchodilation and other smooth muscle relaxation effects.

---

### Question 75: [Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism]
- **Difficulty**: [Medium]
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: [The differential effects of epinephrine on vascular beds mediated by alpha-1 and beta-2 adrenergic receptors, specifically the predominant beta-2 mediated vasodilation in skeletal muscle leading to decreased diastolic blood pressure at low infusion doses. 儿茶酚胺（肾上腺素）对不同血管床的差异性作用，特别是低剂量时β2受体介导的骨骼肌血管扩张导致舒张压降低。]

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 30-year-old healthy male volunteer is participating in a pharmacology study investigating the hemodynamic effects of catecholamines. An intravenous infusion of epinephrine is initiated at a low dose of 0.05 mcg/kg/min. Continuous monitoring via an arterial line reveals the following changes after 5 minutes: systolic blood pressure increases from 118 mmHg to 130 mmHg, diastolic blood pressure decreases from 78 mmHg to 60 mmHg, heart rate increases from 72 bpm to 85 bpm, and mean arterial pressure remains relatively stable at 85 mmHg. The volunteer reports mild palpitations. Which of the following physiological mechanisms best explains the observed decrease in diastolic blood pressure despite the increase in systolic blood pressure?

(A) Increased activation of alpha-1 adrenergic receptors in renal arterioles leading to vasoconstriction and increased renal vascular resistance, which contributes to the overall increase in systemic vascular resistance and diastolic pressure.
(B) Predominant activation of beta-2 adrenergic receptors in the vascular beds of skeletal muscle, causing vasodilation and a significant decrease in total peripheral resistance, particularly affecting diastolic pressure.
(C) Enhanced sympathetic outflow to venous reservoirs, leading to venoconstriction and increased venous return, which increases preload and subsequently raises both systolic and diastolic blood pressure.
(D) Direct chronotropic effect of epinephrine on the sinoatrial node mediated by beta-1 receptors, leading to increased cardiac output, which primarily increases systolic pressure without significantly affecting diastolic pressure.
(E) Stimulation of alpha-2 adrenergic receptors on presynaptic nerve terminals, leading to inhibition of norepinephrine release and a net decrease in sympathetic tone, resulting in vasodilation and decreased blood pressure.

#### Correct Answer: [B]

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 30-year-old healthy male.
- **Intervention:** Low-dose epinephrine infusion (0.05 mcg/kg/min).
- **Hemodynamic Changes:**
    - Systolic BP ↑ (118 -> 130 mmHg).
    - Diastolic BP ↓ (78 -> 60 mmHg).
    - Heart Rate ↑ (72 -> 85 bpm).
    - Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) ≈ stable (85 mmHg).
- **Symptoms:** Mild palpitations.
- **Question:** Mechanism explaining the ↓ diastolic BP despite ↑ systolic BP.

The key observation is the dissociation between systolic and diastolic blood pressure changes: systolic pressure increases, diastolic pressure decreases, and MAP remains stable. This pattern is characteristic of a drug that increases cardiac output (raising systolic pressure) while simultaneously decreasing total peripheral resistance (lowering diastolic pressure). Epinephrine has affinity for both alpha and beta adrenergic receptors. At low doses, the beta-2 effects often predominate.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Epinephrine Receptor Pharmacology:** Epinephrine binds to alpha-1, alpha-2, beta-1, and beta-2 adrenergic receptors. The relative affinity is Beta ≈ Alpha-1 > Alpha-2.
- **Dose-Dependent Effects:**
    - **Low Dose (e.g., 0.05 mcg/kg/min):** Beta-2 receptor activation predominates. Beta-2 receptors are abundant in the vascular smooth muscle of skeletal muscle, coronary arteries, and some other vascular beds. Activation of Beta-2 receptors (Gs coupled) leads to increased intracellular cAMP via adenylyl cyclase activation. cAMP activates Protein Kinase A (PKA), which phosphorylates myosin light chain kinase (MLCK), inhibiting its activity. Reduced MLCK activity leads to decreased smooth muscle contraction and vasodilation. This vasodilation significantly lowers peripheral resistance, particularly in skeletal muscle, leading to a decrease in diastolic blood pressure. Concurrently, beta-1 receptor activation (Gs coupled) increases heart rate (chronotropy) and contractility (inotropy), increasing cardiac output and thus raising systolic blood pressure. The net effect on MAP can be variable but often remains relatively stable or slightly increases due to the increased cardiac output counteracting the decreased peripheral resistance.
    - **High Dose (e.g., > 1 mcg/kg/min):** Alpha-1 receptor activation predominates. Alpha-1 receptors are found on most vascular smooth muscle beds. Activation of Alpha-1 receptors (Gq coupled) leads to activation of phospholipase C (PLC), generating IP3 and DAG. IP3 increases intracellular calcium, and DAG activates PKA. Increased intracellular calcium leads to smooth muscle contraction and vasoconstriction. This vasoconstriction increases total peripheral resistance, significantly raising both systolic and diastolic blood pressure, and MAP. The beta-1 effects still increase heart rate and contractility.
- **Vascular Beds:**
    - **Skeletal Muscle:** Rich in beta-2 receptors. Epinephrine causes vasodilation here, lowering diastolic pressure.
    - **Skin, Splanchnic Circulation:** Also contain beta-2 receptors, contributing to vasodilation.
    - **Renal, Cerebral, Coronary Arteries:** Contain both alpha and beta receptors. The net effect depends on the dose and specific receptor distribution.
    - **Veins:** Contain beta-2 receptors. Epinephrine causes venodilation, decreasing venous return and preload (though this effect is often less pronounced than the arterial effects).
- **Clinical Vignette Application:** The observed hemodynamic profile (↑ systolic BP, ↓ diastolic BP, stable MAP, ↑ HR) is classic for low-dose epinephrine, where beta-2 mediated vasodilation in skeletal muscle is the dominant effect lowering diastolic pressure, while beta-1 mediated increases in heart rate and contractility raise systolic pressure.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the drug and dose:** Low-dose epinephrine infusion.
2.  **Analyze the hemodynamic changes:** Systolic BP ↑, Diastolic BP ↓, MAP stable, HR ↑.
3.  **Recall epinephrine's receptor pharmacology:** Acts on alpha-1, alpha-2, beta-1, beta-2 receptors.
4.  **Consider dose-dependent effects:** Low dose favors beta-2 effects; high dose favors alpha-1 effects.
5.  **Relate receptor activation to vascular effects:**
    - Beta-2 activation → vasodilation (especially skeletal muscle) → ↓ diastolic BP, ↓ TPR.
    - Beta-1 activation → ↑ HR, ↑ contractility → ↑ cardiac output → ↑ systolic BP.
    - Alpha-1 activation → vasoconstriction → ↑ systolic BP, ↑ diastolic BP, ↑ TPR.
6.  **Synthesize the findings:** The observed pattern (↑ systolic, ↓ diastolic, stable MAP) is consistent with predominant beta-2 vasodilation (lowering diastolic pressure) coupled with beta-1 stimulation (raising systolic pressure).
7.  **Evaluate the options based on this synthesis:** Option (B) directly describes the predominant beta-2 vasodilation in skeletal muscle leading to decreased diastolic pressure.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Increased activation of alpha-1 adrenergic receptors in renal arterioles...:** This describes the effect of higher doses of epinephrine or pure alpha-agonists like phenylephrine. Alpha-1 activation causes vasoconstriction, increasing renal vascular resistance and contributing to increased diastolic BP and MAP, contrary to the vignette's findings.
- **(C) Enhanced sympathetic outflow to venous reservoirs...:** While epinephrine can affect venous tone, the primary effect at low doses on arterial resistance is more significant in determining the observed BP changes. Venoconstriction would increase preload, potentially increasing both systolic and diastolic pressures, which doesn't fit the pattern.
- **(D) Direct chronotropic effect...mediated by beta-1 receptors...:** This is true; epinephrine increases heart rate via beta-1 receptors, contributing to the increased systolic pressure. However, it doesn't explain the *decrease* in diastolic pressure, which is the core question. This option only describes part of the overall hemodynamic effect.
- **(E) Stimulation of alpha-2 adrenergic receptors...:** Alpha-2 receptors are primarily presynaptic and mediate negative feedback (inhibiting norepinephrine release). While epinephrine activates alpha-2 receptors, this effect is generally less prominent hemodynamically at low doses compared to the direct alpha-1 and beta-2 effects on vascular smooth muscle and the heart. Furthermore, alpha-2 activation would tend to decrease sympathetic outflow, potentially lowering BP overall, but it doesn't specifically explain the dissociation between systolic and diastolic pressure seen here.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Receptor | Location                                  | G-Protein | Effector Mechanism                                                                 | Primary Vascular Effect                                     | Hemodynamic Effect (Low Dose Epi) |
| :------- | :---------------------------------------- | :-------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| Alpha-1  | Most vascular smooth muscle, iris dilator | Gq        | PLC -> IP3/DAG -> ↑Ca2+                                                              | Vasoconstriction                                             | ↑TPR, ↑SBP, ↑DBP, ↑MAP            |
| Alpha-2  | Presynaptic terminals, platelets, pancreas | Gi        | ↓AC -> ↓cAMP, ↑K+ efflux, ↓Ca2+ influx                                                | Presynaptic inhibition, platelet aggregation, ↓insulin release | Complex, often slight ↑BP          |
| Beta-1   | Heart (SA/AV node, myocardium), kidney JG cells | Gs        | ↑AC -> ↑cAMP -> ↑PKA -> ↑Ca2+ influx (heart), ↑renin release (kidney)               | ↑HR, ↑Contractility, ↑Renin Release                          | ↑CO, ↑SBP, ↑DBP, ↑MAP            |
| Beta-2   | Skeletal muscle vasculature, bronchial smooth muscle, liver, uterus, veins | Gs        | ↑AC -> ↑cAMP -> ↑PKA -> ↓Ca2+ (MLCK phosphorylation/inactivation) | Vasodilation (skeletal muscle, coronary, renal, splanchnic), Bronchodilation, Uterine relaxation, Glycogenolysis | ↓TPR, ↓DBP, ↑HR, ↑SBP, stable MAP |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Low-dose epinephrine primarily stimulates beta-2 adrenergic receptors in skeletal muscle vasculature, causing vasodilation and a decrease in diastolic blood pressure. Simultaneously, beta-1 receptor stimulation increases heart rate and contractility, raising systolic blood pressure. The net effect on mean arterial pressure is often minimal due to the opposing effects on peripheral resistance and cardiac output. Understanding the differential receptor affinities and dose-dependent effects of epinephrine is crucial for interpreting its hemodynamic profile.
